Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse.

I just can't stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Is this patch on your face the only thing that bothers you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. It's really just that one spot. I mean, I'm 52, I work construction out in the sun and the dirt, so... I know I'm not gonna look perfect or anything. I don't really care about the rest of my face or my body. Especially before I head out to work, or... like if I'm going over to my sister's for game night. \n\nI'll just stand in the bathroom and keep checking the mirror. I turn my face back and forth, trying to see how the spot looks under different lights. And I catch myself rubbing at it, too, like maybe I can smooth it out. If I think it looks really obvious that day, I'll pull my hat down real low to try and cast a shadow over it. Or sometimes... I just make up an excuse and don't go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try and prevent people from noticing the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, kind of like I was just saying... the main thing is the hat. I make sure to pull the brim down real low over that side of my face to cast a shadow over it. I figure if the spot is in the dark, the guys at work won't see it. \n\nAnd, uh, if I'm going somewhere where I can't really wear a hat, like inside my sister's house... if I've been in the bathroom checking it in the mirror and rubbing at it, and I decide it looks too obvious that day, I just won't go. I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. Every time I see a photo of myself or look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on. It makes one side of my face look old and just beat-up. My family and friends keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I see it. I just can't stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Is this patch on your face the only thing that bothers you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. It's really just that one spot. I mean, I'm 52, I work construction out in the sun and the dirt, so... I know I'm not gonna look perfect or anything. I don't really care about the rest of my face or my body. It's just... this one sunken patch. If I could just get that fixed or covered up, I'd be completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot, actually. Especially before I head out to work, or... like if I'm going over to my sister's for game night. \n\nI'll just stand in the bathroom and keep checking the mirror. I turn my face back and forth, trying to see how the spot looks under different lights. And I catch myself rubbing at it, too, like maybe I can smooth it out. If I think it looks really obvious that day, I'll pull my hat down real low to try and cast a shadow over it. Or sometimes... I just make up an excuse and don't go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try and prevent people from noticing the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, kind of like I was just saying... the main thing is the hat. I make sure to pull the brim down real low over that side of my face to cast a shadow over it. I figure if the spot is in the dark, the guys at work won't see it. \n\nAnd, uh, if I'm going somewhere where I can't really wear a hat, like inside my sister's house... if I've been in the bathroom checking it in the mirror and rubbing at it, and I decide it looks too obvious that day, I just won't go. I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because... well, I just can't stop looking at this patch on my face. It's rough and sunken-looking. Every time I see a photo of myself or look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on. It makes one side of my face look old and just beat-up. My family and friends keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I see it. I just can't stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Is this patch on your face the only thing that bothers you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. It's really just that one spot. I mean, I'm 52, I work construction out in the sun and the dirt, so... I know I'm not gonna look perfect or anything. I don't really care about the rest of my face or my body. It's just... this one sunken patch. If I could just get that fixed or covered up, I'd be completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot, actually. Especially before I head out to work, or... like if I'm going over to my sister's for game night. \n\nI'll just stand in the bathroom and keep checking the mirror. I turn my face back and forth, trying to see how the spot looks under different lights. And I catch myself rubbing at it, too, like maybe I can smooth it out. If I think it looks really obvious that day, I'll pull my hat down real low to try and cast a shadow over it. Or sometimes... I just make up an excuse and don't go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try and prevent people from noticing the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, kind of like I was just saying... the main thing is the hat. I make sure to pull the brim down real low over that side of my face to cast a shadow over it. I figure if the spot is in the dark, the guys at work won't see it. \n\nAnd, uh, if I'm going somewhere where I can't really wear a hat, like inside my sister's house... if I've been in the bathroom checking it in the mirror and rubbing at it, and I decide it looks too obvious that day, I just won't go. I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because... well, I just can't stop looking at this patch on my face. It's rough and sunken-looking. Every time I see a photo of myself or look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on. It makes one side of my face look old and just beat-up. My family and friends keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I see it. I just can't stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Is this patch on your face the only thing that bothers you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. It's really just that one spot. I mean, I'm 52, I work construction out in the sun and the dirt, so... I know I'm not gonna look perfect or anything. I don't really care about the rest of my face or my body. It's just... this one sunken patch. If I could just get that fixed or covered up, I'd be completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot, actually. Especially before I head out to work, or... like if I'm going over to my sister's for game night. \n\nI'll just stand in the bathroom and keep checking the mirror. I turn my face back and forth, trying to see how the spot looks under different lights. And I catch myself rubbing at it, too, like maybe I can smooth it out. If I think it looks really obvious that day, I'll pull my hat down real low to try and cast a shadow over it. Or sometimes... I just make up an excuse and don't go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try and prevent people from noticing the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, kind of like I was just saying... the main thing is the hat. I make sure to pull the brim down real low over that side of my face to cast a shadow over it. I figure if the spot is in the dark, the guys at work won't see it. \n\nAnd, uh, if I'm going somewhere where I can't really wear a hat, like inside my sister's house... if I've been in the bathroom checking it in the mirror and rubbing at it, and I decide it looks too obvious that day, I just won't go. I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because... well, I just can't stop looking at this patch on my face. It's rough and sunken-looking. Every time I see a photo of myself or look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on. It makes one side of my face look old and just beat-up. My family and friends keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I see it. I just can't stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Is this patch on your face the only thing that bothers you about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. It's really just that one spot. I mean, I'm 52, I work construction out in the sun and the dirt, so... I know I'm not gonna look perfect or anything. I don't really care about the rest of my face or my body. It's just... this one sunken patch. If I could just get that fixed or covered up, I'd be completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot, actually. Especially before I head out to work, or... like if I'm going over to my sister's for game night. \n\nI'll just stand in the bathroom and keep checking the mirror. I turn my face back and forth, trying to see how the spot looks under different lights. And I catch myself rubbing at it, too, like maybe I can smooth it out. If I think it looks really obvious that day, I'll pull my hat down real low to try and cast a shadow over it. Or sometimes... I just make up an excuse and don't go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try and prevent people from noticing the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Well, kind of like I was just saying... the main thing is the hat. I make sure to pull the brim down real low over that side of my face to cast a shadow over it. I figure if the spot is in the dark, the guys at work won't see it. \n\nAnd, uh, if I'm going somewhere where I can't really wear a hat, like inside my sister's house... if I've been in the bathroom checking it in the mirror and rubbing at it, and I decide it looks too obvious that day, I just won't go. I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. I mean, I'm a 52-year-old guy working construction, so it's not like I'm trying to look perfect or anything. The rest of me is just... normal wear and tear, I guess. It's really just this one rough, sunken patch on my face that bothers me. If I could just get rid of that, I'd be completely fine with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face looks as noticeable to others as it does to you?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm just not sure. Like I said before, when I look in the mirror, it's all I see. It looks huge to me. But then... my sister and the guys at work say they don't see it, or it's not a big deal. So, I don't know if it looks as bad to them as it does to me, or if they're just saying that. I really couldn't tell you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the patch on your face, that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying a minute ago, it really is just that one spot. I don't have any other... you know, issues with how I look. I mean, I'm getting older, so obviously I don't look like a kid anymore, but none of that regular stuff bothers me. It's just the patch. That's the only thing I wish I could change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'll call and make up an excuse, say I'm too beat from work or something, just so I don't have to sit there worrying about people staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the patch on your face affected your work or relationships with others in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. I mean, I'm a 52-year-old guy working construction, so it's not like I'm trying to look perfect or anything. The rest of me is just... normal wear and tear, I guess. It's really just this one rough, sunken patch on my face that bothers me. If I could just get rid of that, I'd be completely fine with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face looks as noticeable to others as it does to you?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm just not sure. Like I said before, when I look in the mirror, it's all I see. It looks huge to me. But then... my sister and the guys at work say they don't see it, or it's not a big deal. So, I don't know if it looks as bad to them as it does to me, or if they're just saying that. I really couldn't tell you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the patch on your face, that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying a minute ago, it really is just that one spot. I don't have any other... you know, issues with how I look. I mean, I'm getting older, so obviously I don't look like a kid anymore, but none of that regular stuff bothers me. It's just the patch. That's the only thing I wish I could change.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the patch on your face is actually there, or is it something that you see that others do not?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I know I see it. It looks completely real to me when I'm standing there looking in the mirror. But since the guys at work and my sister swear they don't see anything, it gets confusing. \n\nI honestly don't know anymore if it's actually there the way I think it is, or if it's just something only I can see. I just know what I see, but I really couldn't tell you for sure what's going on with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. It's really just this one rough, sunken patch on my face that bothers me. If I could just get rid of that, I'd be completely fine with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face looks as noticeable to others as it does to you?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm just not sure. So, I don't know if it looks as bad to them as it does to me, or if they're just saying that. I really couldn't tell you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the patch on your face, that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying a minute ago, it really is just that one spot. I know I see it. It looks completely real to me when I'm standing there looking in the mirror. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. I mean, I'm a 52-year-old guy working construction, so it's not like I'm trying to look perfect or anything. The rest of me is just... normal wear and tear, I guess. It's really just this one rough, sunken patch on my face that bothers me. If I could just get rid of that, I'd be completely fine with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face looks as noticeable to others as it does to you?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm just not sure. Like I said before, when I look in the mirror, it's all I see. It looks huge to me. But then... my sister and the guys at work say they don't see it, or it's not a big deal. So, I don't know if it looks as bad to them as it does to me, or if they're just saying that. I really couldn't tell you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the patch on your face, that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying a minute ago, it really is just that one spot. I don't have any other... you know, issues with how I look. I mean, I'm getting older, so obviously I don't look like a kid anymore, but none of that regular stuff bothers me. It's just the patch. That's the only thing I wish I could change.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the patch on your face is actually there, or is it something that you see that others do not?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I know I see it. It looks completely real to me when I'm standing there looking in the mirror. But since the guys at work and my sister swear they don't see anything, it gets confusing. \n\nI honestly don't know anymore if it's actually there the way I think it is, or if it's just something only I can see. I just know what I see, but I really couldn't tell you for sure what's going on with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that would be visible in a photograph if you weren't trying to hide it?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, I try to avoid pictures altogether because of it. I guess... if I took one without covering it up, maybe it would show up? But since people keep telling me they don't see it in person, I don't know if a camera would pick it up either. I haven't really tested it out to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that would be noticeable to a doctor or a dermatologist if they examined you?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I haven't actually been to a skin doctor or anyone like that to have them look at it. I mean... if the people I'm around every day don't see it, maybe a doctor wouldn't either. Or maybe they would, since they're trained and know what to look for. I just honestly don't know what they would see if they examined me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, like I said, I've skipped out on my sister's game nights a few times now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me. I just make up an excuse. \n\nAnd at work... well, it's definitely made me late a few times. I get stuck in the bathroom checking the mirror, trying to get my hat sitting just right to hide it, and before I know it, I'm running behind. I've also started avoiding the guys when they take site photos. You know, when a job is done and everyone wants a group picture? I just sort of duck out or find something else to do. I just don't want that patch immortalized in a photo where anyone can stare at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the patch on your face something that you think about all the time, or are there specific situations that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time, honestly. I just can't stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face. Every time I look in the mirror or see a photo, it's right there. It makes one side of my face look so old and beat-up. My sister and some of the guys at work keep telling me they barely notice anything, but... I don't know, it's all I can think about. It's always on my mind, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say this concern about the patch on your face is causing you?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... it's causing me a lot of stress, I guess. I mean, it's just always there, bothering me. It makes me feel embarrassed and, you know, just bad about how I look. It's frustrating to deal with it every day, especially when it messes with my work or seeing my family. So yeah, it definitely gets to me a fair amount.\n\nDoctor: When you look at the patch on your face, do you think it looks worse to you than it would to someone else looking at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean... I know what my sister and the guys at work say, about not really noticing it. But I don't know if it looks worse to me than it does to them. I can't really tell what they're seeing. I just know that when I look in the mirror, it looks pretty bad to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if someone else looked closely at your face, they would see the patch as a significant flaw?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean... if they got right up in my face and stared at it, I don't know if they'd think it was a big deal or not. Like I said, my sister and the guys on the crew say they don't notice it, so... I just don't know what goes through other people's heads when they look at me. I really don't know what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that other people can see, or is it something that only you notice?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, people keep telling me they barely notice anything. But for me... I just can't stop seeing it. Whenever I look in the mirror, or if I see a photo, it's right there. It's this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face, and it just makes that whole side of me look old and beat-up. So even if other people say they don't really see it, it's pretty much all I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is actually noticeable to other people when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but... I really just don't know. If I go by what my sister and the guys at work tell me, then I guess it's not noticeable to them. But I don't know if they're just being nice or what. I can't see it through their eyes, so I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were able to completely hide or fix the patch on your face, your life and daily activities would go back to normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really think it would. I mean, right now, it's getting in the way of a lot of things. If I could just fix it... well, I wouldn't be running late getting out the door for my construction jobs anymore. And I wouldn't have to keep dodging site photos with the crew. I've even started skipping out on some of our family game nights lately just because... I just don't want people looking at my face. So, yeah, if it was gone, I think my life would definitely get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Is the patch on your face the only flaw you're concerned about, or are there other aspects of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: No, it's pretty much just that. I mean, I'm a 52-year-old guy working construction, so it's not like I'm trying to look perfect or anything. The rest of me is just... normal wear and tear, I guess. It's really just this one rough, sunken patch on my face that bothers me. If I could just get rid of that, I'd be completely fine with how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face looks as noticeable to others as it does to you?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm just not sure. Like I said before, when I look in the mirror, it's all I see. It looks huge to me. But then... my sister and the guys at work say they don't see it, or it's not a big deal. So, I don't know if it looks as bad to them as it does to me, or if they're just saying that. I really couldn't tell you.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the patch on your face, that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying a minute ago, it really is just that one spot. I don't have any other... you know, issues with how I look. I mean, I'm getting older, so obviously I don't look like a kid anymore, but none of that regular stuff bothers me. It's just the patch. That's the only thing I wish I could change.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the patch on your face is actually there, or is it something that you see that others do not?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I know I see it. It looks completely real to me when I'm standing there looking in the mirror. But since the guys at work and my sister swear they don't see anything, it gets confusing. \n\nI honestly don't know anymore if it's actually there the way I think it is, or if it's just something only I can see. I just know what I see, but I really couldn't tell you for sure what's going on with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that would be visible in a photograph if you weren't trying to hide it?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, I try to avoid pictures altogether because of it. I guess... if I took one without covering it up, maybe it would show up? But since people keep telling me they don't see it in person, I don't know if a camera would pick it up either. I haven't really tested it out to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that would be noticeable to a doctor or a dermatologist if they examined you?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I haven't actually been to a skin doctor or anyone like that to have them look at it. I mean... if the people I'm around every day don't see it, maybe a doctor wouldn't either. Or maybe they would, since they're trained and know what to look for. I just honestly don't know what they would see if they examined me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face is something that would be visible to someone else if you pointed it out to them?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that either. I've never actually tried pointing it out to anyone. I'm usually just trying to hide it, you know? \n\nI guess... if I pointed right at the exact spot, maybe they'd finally see what I'm seeing. But then again, if they're already looking at my face and telling me there's nothing there, pointing at it might not change anything. I just really don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
